## Production schema evidence

**VERIFIED in read-only Production catalog inspection on 16 October 2026:**

- PostgreSQL 17.6; the inspection transaction was explicitly read-only and identified project `cuowwlduhxxifmpvpltt`.
- Core orders, order items, payments, payment allocations, payment corrections, refunds, Fresh Pick stock/events, staff profiles, and migration-history structures exist.
- Relevant RLS is enabled. Catalog inspection found staff-profile self-read policy and read policies for financial tables; payment-correction reads are role-gated. This is not a complete behavioral security test.
- Existing RPC signatures include `record_payment_correction`, `record_overpayment_refund`, `cancel_and_refund_paid_fresh_pick`, Fresh Pick Ready-for-Collection mark/undo, and guest checkout functions.
- Existing correction, overpayment, Fresh Pick cancellation, and Ready-for-Collection functions are SECURITY DEFINER under `postgres` ownership. Their recorded execute ACLs include the existing application roles. Function bodies and full behavioral equivalence were not inspected.
- Installed extensions observed include `btree_gist`, `citext`, `pg_cron`, `pg_stat_statements`, `pgcrypto`, `plpgsql`, `supabase_vault`, and `uuid-ossp`.

**VERIFIED differences from accepted DEV requirements:**

- Production `public.refunds` does not have `refund_method`, `refund_method_description`, or `request_key`.
- Production does not expose the DEV Refund Recording V1 RPCs `record_order_refund`, `record_overpayment_refund_with_details`, or `cancel_and_refund_paid_fresh_pick_with_details`.
- Production does have payment-correction, legacy overpayment-refund, Fresh Pick cancellation/refund, and stock structures needed by existing workflows.

**VERIFIED from the October 14 schema-only snapshot:**

- The schema SQL defines 111 tables: 64 in `public`, 27 in `auth`, 11 in `realtime`, 8 in `storage`, and 1 in `supabase_migrations`. It also includes schema definitions for `extensions`, `graphql`, `graphql_public`, `pgbouncer`, `staff_preorder_private`, and `vault` (plus built-in `public`). `staff_preorder_private` has no application tables in this snapshot.
- The archive listing contains schema object entries for 309 functions, 161 policies, 251 indexes, 150 constraints, 168 foreign keys, 76 triggers (68 ordinary and 8 constraint triggers), 7 extensions, 7 sequences, and 31 types. The schema SQL contains function bodies and RLS policy definitions. No views are defined in the supplied schema SQL.
- The extracted SQL and archive listing contain no ACL entries, `GRANT`, or `REVOKE` statements. Thus function owners/security attributes and policies can be inspected, but the snapshot cannot establish the complete table/function privilege grants. Prior 16 October catalog findings for selected RPC grants remain separate evidence.
- The `auth` schema structure is present, but Auth project configuration, Auth users, passkey registrations/configuration, Storage object metadata rows, and Storage object bytes are not provided by this schema-only extraction. No records were read.
- Key table structures include `orders`, `order_items`, `payments`, `payment_allocations`, `payment_corrections`, `refunds`, `order_timeline_events`, `extra_stock`, `extra_stock_events`, and `staff_profiles`. RLS is enabled on the relevant tables; the snapshot includes the policies but not the grants needed for a complete security reconstruction.

## Release blockers and remaining risks

1. **BLOCKER — Trustworthy application source baseline unavailable.** The current Production CLI deployment has no verified source commit/tree. A minimal patch and selective integration both need a trustworthy base; full accepted-DEV replacement is not safe. Historical source recovery is closed; do not repeat it. An owner-designated maintained source snapshot for the next release is still needed.
2. **HIGH / BLOCKER FOR THE REFUND MIGRATION — Existing Production application call sites are unknown.** The reviewed migration revokes direct `anon`/`authenticated` INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE on `public.refunds`. No direct refund-table writes were found in the accepted DEV source, but the current Production application is not recoverable and external integrations/operational scripts remain unverified. The permission change could affect an unknown existing caller; confirm compatibility before execution.
3. **HIGH — Full disaster recovery remains unverified.** The October 14 archive was only partially restored locally; Supabase-managed dependencies, roles/ownership/ACLs were not fully reproduced. Its data is older than current Production and it must never be restored over live Production. Application rollback does not reverse the migration; a current-state recovery plan and explicit acceptance of remaining rehearsal limits are required before the database change.
4. **HIGH — Effective Production deployment configuration is not verified.** Project identity of prior read-only database access is known, but the current Production deployment's effective Supabase target, build overrides, and external schedules were not independently confirmed. Verify deployment-scoped configuration without revealing secret values before any release.
5. **MEDIUM / MONITOR — Intermittent timeouts remain unexplained.** DEV Vercel timeouts were observed on 16 October; a later single owner-run Mac Safari check succeeded for the October collection and Thai Milk Tea Mango detail. Production staff Home also had a real transient transaction-abort/statement-timeout error and later recovered. Neither incident's root cause is known. A single recovered event does not prove a persistent defect, but require a healthy pre-release window and targeted post-release monitoring; stop if the symptom recurs during release.
6. **PENDING — Application compatibility and non-regression evidence.** Refund migration SQL passed the bounded compatibility/security review and isolated tests, but that is not end-to-end validation of the accepted application against current Production data/configuration. Once a trustworthy baseline exists, test the smallest feature slice with synthetic data in isolation and perform read-only Production acceptance checks.

**Completed, do not repeat absent new evidence:** Production-specific Refund Recording V1 draft, permission/RLS review, independent review, synthetic cases, isolated October 14 snapshot rehearsal, and read-only Production schema preflight. The reviewed draft checksum is `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`; its migration compatibility gate was READY, but it has not been executed and execution still requires separate explicit owner authorization.

## Production-specific Refund Recording V1 preparation

**PENDING approval; no hosted changes.** The original draft at `/private/tmp/project-flour-production-refund-recording-v1-draft.sql` (SHA-256 `fc010e4ff5719ed526a0bf580c72c70f5876088a3232d0cc33c92744863ce725`) was revised after the current Production ACL audit; its corrected hash and local rehearsal are recorded in “Security correction rehearsal” below. The draft adds the three nullable refund metadata columns, method/type checks and request-key unique index, replaces the existing refund validator with the accepted V1 logic, and adds only the three V1 RPCs. It preserves existing Production RLS policies, `record_payment_correction`, legacy refund RPC definitions/ACLs, and both Fresh Pick checkout overloads. It now also removes direct client-role INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE on `public.refunds` while preserving SELECT. It omits the new reason-length table constraint; the new RPC validates reasons while legacy reason data remains uninspected.

**VERIFIED in the disposable local PostgreSQL 17.11 rehearsal:** The October 14 schema-only SQL restored successfully after omitting unavailable Supabase-managed extension definitions (`pg_cron`, `supabase_vault`), the `pg_stat_statements` extension, and a platform event-trigger ownership statement that local PostgreSQL cannot reproduce. The migration draft applied successfully in one transaction. Synthetic Owner/Manager/Crew tests passed: general refund authorization and receipt limits, invalid method/description/future-date rejection, Fresh Pick misuse rejection, idempotent retry and conflicting request-key rejection, concurrent identical requests returning the same refund ID, overpayment wrapper and legacy overpayment path with the overpayment-specific cap, legacy Fresh Pick cancellation/refund and its V1 metadata-aware wrapper, and Manager denial on the Owner-only Fresh Pick RPC. General refund tests also confirmed the synthetic order, payment, and allocation remained unchanged. The original Production validator body in the October 14 snapshot matches the accepted DEV pre-V1 validator; the V1 replacement adds the `order_refund` remaining-receipts cap. The existing `record_payment_correction`, legacy overpayment, and legacy Fresh Pick function definition hashes were unchanged; the existing refund policies and a synthetic refund-table ACL sentinel were unchanged. Both `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads remained present. New RPC execute grants matched the draft: no `anon` execute; authenticated for the general and Fresh Pick RPCs; authenticated plus service role for the overpayment details RPC.

**VERIFIED difference:** The October 14 Production refund validator body matches the accepted DEV pre-V1 Fresh Pick validator after normalization. V1 adds the `order_refund` remaining-receipts cap while retaining the Fresh Pick and overpayment branches. Existing Production records were not present in the schema-only reconstruction; no claim is made about data-level compatibility.

**LIMITATIONS / UNKNOWN:** The schema dump contains no ACL entries or table data. Synthetic ACL sentinels demonstrate that the draft does not change existing grants; they do not establish current Production grants. The local reconstruction omitted the platform components noted above and does not test hosted Auth configuration, external notifications, application/browser integration, or current 16 October Production state. No full Production backup restore rehearsal is confirmed.

The authorized read-only Production refund-table and RPC ACL inspection is recorded below. The audit does not replace the separate recovery rehearsal or the requirement for explicit approval before any Production migration.

## Production refund permission audit — 16 October 2026

**VERIFIED by read-only catalog inspection:** The Supabase management connection identified the target as `project-flour-prod`, reference `cuowwlduhxxifmpvpltt`, PostgreSQL 17.6. Catalog reads ran in a transaction whose `transaction_read_only` setting was `on`. No table rows were queried and no database objects or permissions were changed.

- `public.refunds` is owned by `postgres`; RLS is enabled, `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` is off. The ACL grants table-level SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE, REFERENCES, TRIGGER, and MAINTAIN to `anon`, `authenticated`, `service_role`, and `postgres`; no column-level ACL entries were present. `supabase_admin` also has effective access through its administrative privilege.
- The only refund policy found is permissive `SELECT` for `authenticated` with `USING (true)`. No INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, or ALL policy exists. `anon` has no refund policy. Thus ordinary `anon` and `authenticated` INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE statements are denied by RLS despite their table ACL grants. Their read access is likewise constrained by RLS (authenticated has the SELECT policy; anon does not).
- **Confirmed direct-write risk:** `anon` and `authenticated` also have `TRUNCATE` table privilege. PostgreSQL TRUNCATE is not governed by RLS, so any SQL execution path that can issue it under either role could bypass refund RPCs and erase the table. The ordinary Supabase REST table API does not provide a general SQL/TRUNCATE endpoint, but the catalog grant is still an unsafe privilege to preserve. In addition, `service_role` has all table DML grants and `BYPASSRLS`, so it can directly insert/update/delete/truncate without RPC validation. `postgres` also has `BYPASSRLS` and owns the table; `supabase_admin` is superuser. No inspection established whether deployed backends use service-role direct refund-table writes.
- Existing `record_overpayment_refund` and `cancel_and_refund_paid_fresh_pick` signatures remain executable by `authenticated`, `service_role`, and `postgres`, not `anon`. `record_payment_correction` has the same execute-role set. The refund aggregate helper `order_refunds_total(uuid)` is executable by `PUBLIC`, `anon`, `authenticated`, `service_role`, and `postgres`; this is pre-existing and was not changed. The new V1 RPCs are absent in Production at audit time.
- The approved draft SHA-256 remains `fc010e4ff5719ed526a0bf580c72c70f5876088a3232d0cc33c92744863ce725`. It leaves current table ACLs and RLS untouched; it revokes broad execute defaults on each new RPC and grants `authenticated` for general/Fresh Pick recording, and `authenticated` plus `service_role` for the metadata-aware overpayment wrapper. It does not change any existing RPC ACL.

**ASSESSMENT:** Preserving all current grants in the draft is **not recommended** because it preserves `TRUNCATE` for `anon` and `authenticated`, which RLS cannot prevent. Minimum permission correction before release: revoke direct `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, and `TRUNCATE` on `public.refunds` from `anon` and `authenticated`; the current policies already prevent the first three, while removing TRUNCATE closes the RLS bypass. This should not affect SECURITY DEFINER refund RPCs owned by `postgres`, but must be included in the Production-specific draft and re-rehearsed before approval. `service_role` remains a trusted privileged bypass; whether to revoke its direct DML/TRUNCATE requires a separate backend call-site review so existing trusted workflows are not broken. The current audit cannot establish whether that key is used for direct refund writes.

**UNKNOWN:** Effective use of the service-role key by deployed backends; whether any direct refund-table writes are relied upon; whether any non-REST SQL execution path is exposed to anon/authenticated; production behavior beyond catalog ACL/RLS metadata; and current state after this inspection. No permissions were changed. At the time of that audit the original draft hash matched; it was subsequently corrected and rehearsed as recorded below.

**Previous next action — completed:** The Production-specific draft was revised to revoke direct refund-table `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, and `TRUNCATE` from `anon` and `authenticated`; the local PostgreSQL 17.11 rehearsal and regression results follow. Service-role call-site review remains pending. Do not run any Production migration without separate explicit approval.

## Security correction rehearsal — 16 October 2026

**VERIFIED:** Updated draft `/private/tmp/project-flour-production-refund-recording-v1-draft.sql`, SHA-256 `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`. Added exactly `REVOKE INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE ON TABLE public.refunds FROM anon, authenticated;`. SELECT, service-role, `postgres`, and `supabase_admin` privileges are not revoked. The draft does not modify policies or unrelated tables.

**VERIFIED in isolated PostgreSQL 17.11:** Replayed the October 14 schema-only Production snapshot in a local Unix-socket-only disposable cluster. As previously documented, platform-only/unavailable pieces were omitted (`pg_cron`, `supabase_vault`, `pg_stat_statements`, and Supabase event triggers); schema-only files contain no Production records or ACLs. Synthetic roles and the audited Production refund-table ACL baseline were used, including `BYPASSRLS` for `service_role`. The draft applied successfully as one transaction under the local `postgres` role. All three new V1 RPCs were confirmed owned by `postgres`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, and configured with an empty search path.

**Executed regression results:**

- `anon` and `authenticated`: effective INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE privileges are false after migration; SELECT remains true. No PUBLIC write ACL remained, and effective privilege checks included inherited-role evaluation. Existing non-write grants were preserved. `service_role` direct-write privileges were unchanged.
- Authenticated SELECT still executes under the unchanged refund SELECT policy.
- Synthetic Owner and Manager successfully recorded general refunds through the new RPC; synthetic Crew was denied. Over-limit refund was rejected.
- Same-key retry returned the same refund ID; reuse of the key with changed details was rejected. Two concurrent same-key calls returned one ID and created one row.
- The metadata-aware overpayment wrapper and legacy overpayment RPC both succeeded on synthetic orders.
- The metadata-aware Fresh Pick refund and existing legacy Fresh Pick cancellation/refund both succeeded for the synthetic Owner; Manager was rejected by the Owner-only wrapper. Expected stock release occurred only for the synthetic Fresh Pick rows.
- Existing `record_payment_correction` executed successfully on a synthetic order. Its function-definition/ACL fingerprint remained unchanged.
- Both `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads remained present with unchanged definition/ACL fingerprints. The existing refund RLS policy fingerprint and protected payment/legacy refund/Fresh Pick function fingerprints remained unchanged.
- General refund calls left their synthetic order status, payment records, and payment allocations unchanged.

**LIMITATIONS / UNKNOWN:** This is an isolated rehearsal against the reconstructed October 14 schema, not a current Production execution. ACLs were recreated from the 16 October catalog audit because the schema snapshot excludes grants. Auth configuration, Supabase-managed components, deployed service-role call sites, and external integrations were not tested. `service_role` retains direct write privileges and `BYPASSRLS`; whether deployed code relies on those privileges remains unknown. No Production or hosted DEV changes occurred.

**Next exact action:** Have a second reviewer inspect the corrected draft and isolated regression evidence, with particular attention to the separate decision about trusted `service_role` direct writes. No Production migration execution is authorized by this rehearsal.

## Independent final review — 16 October 2026

**VERIFIED:** The Production-specific draft at `/private/tmp/project-flour-production-refund-recording-v1-draft.sql` matches SHA-256 `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`. The reviewed test plan is `/private/tmp/project-flour-production-refund-v1-isolated-test-plan.md` (SHA-256 `beeab9ce6f1bc0e042e8d020c048a0531326baa20b852cc7199247aad9ab36f5`). The SQL was read in full. No hosted database was contacted.

**SECURITY REVIEW:** The added table revoke removes `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, and `TRUNCATE` for `anon` and `authenticated`, leaving SELECT intact. Earlier catalog evidence recorded no PUBLIC table ACL and no inherited path that preserves those client-role writes; the isolated rehearsal reports effective client DML/TRUNCATE privileges false. `service_role` remains a deliberate privileged exception with direct table write/TRUNCATE and `BYPASSRLS`; `postgres` ownership and administrative access also remain. The draft does not widen RLS or change existing policies. The three new routines are SECURITY DEFINER with empty search paths, static SQL, explicit `public`/`auth` references, and grants limited to `authenticated` for general and Fresh Pick workflows, and `authenticated` plus `service_role` for the overpayment wrapper. The actor helper binds authenticated callers to `auth.uid()` and checks active staff; role checks enforce Owner/Manager or Owner as intended. For `service_role`, the helper accepts a caller-supplied active staff ID, after which the role check still requires Owner/Manager for overpayment. This is a trusted-service capability, not an authenticated-user impersonation path; the key must remain server-only. No dynamic SQL was found. Deployment must apply as the intended database owner and verify function owners and execute ACLs afterward.

**APPLICATION CALL-SITE REVIEW:** The accepted source commit `c6105eec0bbee64b86abcd45bb9be71f9eb2deeb` was searched directly. Refund writes in `src/workspaces/owner/orders/actions.ts` use the three RPCs; no direct `.from('refunds').insert/update/upsert/delete` call was found in the inspected application source. Refund table access in order and report query modules is SELECT-only. `createServiceClient` is used for narrow `staff_profiles(id, display_name)` attribution lookups in the inspected order/report flows, not refund writes. This finding is limited to the inspected source; it does not establish absence of external integrations or untracked operational scripts. The app-level search therefore found no service-role refund write dependency, while external use remains UNKNOWN.

**REGRESSION REVIEW:** The draft adds nullable metadata, a partial unique request-key index, method/type checks, and extends only the existing refund validator. The prior Production validator’s overpayment and Fresh Pick rules are retained in the replacement body; the trigger remains an INSERT trigger in the October 14 schema. The draft does not replace `record_payment_correction`, either legacy refund RPC, either checkout overload, existing order/payment/refund rows, or RLS policies. Existing legacy RPCs remain callable and may still create rows without new method/idempotency metadata by design; the new metadata and idempotency guarantees apply to the new wrappers, not every legacy call path.

**TEST EVIDENCE AND LIMITATIONS:** The test plan documents an isolated PostgreSQL 17.11 rehearsal, synthetic Production-shaped schema/roles, and the platform omissions already recorded above. The Master Status contains the prior run’s summarized results: migration transaction success; effective grants; Owner/Manager versus Crew authorization; idempotency/concurrency; legacy and metadata-aware overpayment and Fresh Pick paths; unchanged payment correction, checkout overloads, policies, and protected function fingerprints; and no changes to synthetic orders/payments/allocations for general refunds. I did not rerun that rehearsal, and no raw test log was available among the inspected artifacts. The rehearsal is not evidence of current Production data compatibility or external service-role integrations. The plan does not explicitly enumerate focused date-boundary/future-date tests or every method-description edge case; the SQL checks these, but those cases should be added to the local regression evidence before execution. October 14 schema-only data cannot independently verify all existing row values, and the snapshot is not the current October 16 catalog.

**REVIEW CLASSIFICATION:**

- **MEDIUM —** `service_role` retains direct refund-table writes and `BYPASSRLS`; no such writes were found in the inspected accepted app source, but external integrations remain unknown. This migration leaves that privileged capability unchanged.
- **MEDIUM —** Test output is summarized in this status rather than preserved as raw logs; several input/date edge cases are not explicit in the test plan.
- **LOW —** Existing legacy refund RPCs remain available for compatibility and can create legacy rows without the new metadata/idempotency behavior.
- **INFORMATIONAL —** The pre-existing `order_refunds_total(uuid)` helper has PUBLIC execution according to the earlier audit; this draft does not change it.

**READINESS:** **A — Ready for Production approval review only.** The static review found no migration defect requiring another local SQL correction. This is not deployment approval: current Production state, external service-role usage, actual Production data compatibility, and recovery readiness remain separate gates. No database or application changes were made.

**Next exact action:** Owner reviews the corrected draft and this independent assessment for a separate Production migration decision; do not execute the migration until that decision and the outstanding recovery/current-state gates are satisfied.

## Isolated restore and final validation — 16 October 2026

**Backup and draft integrity — VERIFIED:** The owner-provided plaintext archive was accessible at `/private/tmp/project-flour-restore.Pwg7Qv/production-20261014.restore-work.dump` (2,656,605 bytes); `pg_restore --list` and a local TOC read succeeded. The encrypted copy remains at the same temporary directory and still matches SHA-256 `42f1b2173891850899ff726008ccfbcc3645cfbaf494b2e62db190add3b3cde6`. The Production-specific migration draft remains unchanged at SHA-256 `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`.

**Local recovery rehearsal — PARTIAL, NOT A COMPLETE RECOVERY VERIFICATION:** PostgreSQL 17.11 ran in a newly initialized private temporary cluster, reachable only through its private Unix socket with TCP disabled. A new local database was created; the archive was restored without replaying Production ownership or ACLs. `pg_restore` returned nonzero with 9 error lines and 3 warning lines. Missing `pg_cron` and `supabase_vault` extensions prevented restoration of related cron objects/sequences and the vault secrets object/data. The restored database has the core `public`, Auth, Storage, and migration-history structures; aggregate row counts were public 2,559, Auth 677, Storage 108, and migration history 151. Structural aggregate checks found 517 functions, 75 triggers, 161 RLS policies across 88 RLS-enabled tables, 531 constraints, and 363 indexes in the checked application/platform schemas. The relevant Auth users and Storage objects tables were present. These are counts in the local restored copy only, not a comparison with live Production.

**Restore limitations:** The archive TOC listed 113 table-data entries and no ACL/default-ACL entries. This rehearsal used `--no-owner --no-acl`; consequently Production object ownership and grants were not reconstructed. Storage object bytes are not part of a database dump, and Supabase Auth/project configuration is external to the database. Because `pg_cron` and `supabase_vault` dependencies were unavailable and restore exited with errors, this does not establish a complete or independently recoverable Supabase project backup. The SQL migration applied successfully in one transaction to the isolated restored copy, including the restored October 14 rows; this is local compatibility evidence only.

**Additional refund input tests — 16/16 PASS:** The actual `record_order_refund` RPC was exercised in the isolated database with synthetic UUIDs and values. No refund row was inserted; the synthetic request-key count remained zero. For valid inputs, reaching the expected `Not authenticated` guard confirms earlier input validation accepted them; it does not represent a successful end-to-end refund recording test.

| Case | Expected | Actual |
|---|---|---|
| Early historical date `0001-01-01` | Accepted by date validation (no business lower bound is defined) | Passed date validation |
| Malaysia current date `2026-10-16` | Accepted; upper bound inclusive | Passed date validation |
| Future date `2026-10-17` | Rejected | Rejected with the expected future-date validation |
| `others` method with NULL, empty, or whitespace description | Rejected | All three rejected as missing description |
| Standard method with a nonempty description | Rejected | Rejected as description only allowed for Other |
| Standard method with NULL, empty, or whitespace description | Accepted/normalized to NULL | All three passed method validation |
| `others` with a valid description | Accepted | Passed method validation |
| `others` description length 200 / 201 | 200 accepted; 201 rejected | Both expected outcomes |
| Reason NULL, empty, or whitespace | Rejected | All three rejected as missing reason |

The migration was not changed. The exact PostgreSQL `date` type minimum was not tested; the SQL draft defines no application-level lower date bound, and the early ISO date above was the tested historical value.

**Cleanup — VERIFIED:** The disposable database and local cluster directory were removed. The decrypted plaintext archive was removed. The encrypted copy remains and its checksum was rechecked after cleanup. The original Documents and Google Drive backups were not touched. No hosted connection, Production/DEV change, deployment, application-code change, commit, or push occurred.

## Restore error assessment and Refund V1 release gate — 16 October 2026

The available record does not preserve the individual text or ordering of all nine errors, so they cannot be safely enumerated beyond those categories. Of the 3 warning lines, one was the restore summary noting errors were ignored; the other two are not individually recorded. No details are inferred here.

**Refund Recording V1 impact — READY for the bounded migration-compatibility gate:** The identified failures concern cron/vault objects absent from the local environment. The reviewed Refund V1 migration does not reference those objects, and it applied successfully in one transaction to the isolated October 14 restored database, including its restored records. The separate synthetic refund input validation cases passed 16/16. This supports compatibility with the restored October 14 schema/data for the tested migration scope; it does not establish current October 16 Production parity, reproduce Production ACLs/ownership, prove full end-to-end application behavior, or authorize Production execution. The restore errors themselves are not evidence of a Refund V1 dependency failure. No dependency on cron or vault was established for refunds, its RPCs/validator, staff authorization, payment corrections, legacy or Fresh Pick refunds, or checkout by the migration review and local application test. This is bounded evidence, not a claim that every live function body is equivalent.

**Full Supabase disaster recovery — BLOCKED:** The partial restore is not a complete recovery rehearsal. A realistic recovery also requires compatible PostgreSQL extensions (`pg_cron` and `supabase_vault` are specifically evidenced as missing here), required database roles and memberships, ownership and grants (not present in the archive TOC and deliberately skipped with `--no-owner --no-acl`), Auth/project configuration outside the database dump, Storage object bytes outside the database, and relevant Supabase project configuration and managed services. Their exact recoverability was not verified. The October 14 backup remains for isolated testing only; Production remains authoritative for all current business records. Never restore historical backup data over Production or synchronize DEV catalogue/order data into it.

**Separate gate decision:** (A) Refund Recording V1 migration compatibility against the tested October 14 schema/data: **READY**, with the limitations stated above and subject to a distinct Production preflight and explicit execution approval. (B) Full Production disaster recovery readiness: **BLOCKED**. The latter does not make the identified cron/vault restore errors a demonstrated V1 migration dependency, but the incomplete rehearsal must not be represented as full recovery protection.

**Next exact action:** For the Refund V1 release path, prepare a separate, explicitly authorized Production preflight of the exact reviewed migration (including current project identity, unapplied version, and the approved draft checksum); keep the incomplete disaster-recovery rehearsal as a separate blocker for claiming full recovery readiness. No Production access or execution is authorized by this assessment.

## Refund Recording V1 Production preflight — read-only — 16 October 2026

**Authorization and target — VERIFIED:** Existing owner authorization explicitly permits read-only Production schema, function, policy, grant, and migration-history inspection. Supabase project metadata identifies `cuowwlduhxxifmpvpltt` as `project-flour-prod`, ACTIVE_HEALTHY, PostgreSQL 17.6.1.166; a read-only database query confirmed PostgreSQL 17.6. No Production data records were queried.

**Migration artifact — VERIFIED:** `/private/tmp/project-flour-production-refund-recording-v1-draft.sql` is 18,028 bytes and matches SHA-256 `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`.

**Current schema and history — VERIFIED:** Version `20261015010528` is absent from `supabase_migrations.schema_migrations`. All three V1 RPCs, the three refund metadata columns, the request-key unique index, and the `order_refund` constraint support are absent, as expected before this migration. The existing validator and its refund trigger are present. The current refunds columns, existing constraints/indexes, trigger, and authenticated SELECT-only RLS policy match the October 14 schema snapshot. Function-body hashes for the validator, refund total/receipt helpers, legacy overpayment refund, payment correction, legacy Fresh Pick cancellation/refund, actor/role helpers, and both `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads match the October 14 snapshot. No relevant definition drift was found among those inspected objects. The October 14 archive has no ACL entries, so a historical ACL comparison is unavailable.

**Current privileges — VERIFIED:** `public.refunds` is owned by `postgres`, RLS is enabled, FORCE RLS is off, and its only policy is authenticated SELECT (`USING true`). There are no column ACL entries or PUBLIC grants, and no inherited role memberships for `anon`, `authenticated`, or `service_role` were found. The live table ACL grants SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, and TRUNCATE directly to both `anon` and `authenticated`; effective checks confirm those privileges. With no DML RLS policies, ordinary client-role DML is subject to RLS default-deny, but TRUNCATE is not governed by RLS. The reviewed draft revokes INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE from `anon` and `authenticated` while retaining SELECT; it leaves `service_role` and `postgres` privileges intact. `service_role` retains direct table privileges and BYPASSRLS.

**RPC grants — VERIFIED:** Legacy `record_overpayment_refund`, `cancel_and_refund_paid_fresh_pick`, and `record_payment_correction` remain callable by `authenticated` and `service_role`; both `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads remain callable by `anon`, `authenticated`, and `service_role`. The three new V1 routines are absent. The existing public receipt/refund total helper remains callable as before. No existing policy or protected function definition was changed by this inspection.

**Preflight outcome:** The draft checksum is correct, the target identity/version match, the migration is unapplied, and inspected schema/function definitions have no unexpected October 14-to-current drift. Current client-role ACLs are the known condition the reviewed migration addresses. This is a read-only preflight result only; it does not authorize migration execution. Full disaster recovery remains BLOCKED as recorded above, and the October 14 backup must never replace or synchronize live Production business records.

**Next exact action:** Request separate explicit owner authorization to execute only the checksum-verified Refund Recording V1 migration on Production after an immediate repeat of project identity, migration-version-absent, and reviewed-object prechecks. No migration, data write, deployment, commit, or push was performed in this preflight.

**Release-scope minimization:** The least risky approach is **B — selective feature integration onto an exact, verified Production source baseline**, with one independently tested feature slice at a time. That baseline is currently unavailable, so no safe selective patch can be prepared now. **A — a minimal patch** still needs a trustworthy application base and could overwrite unknown Production behavior. **C — deploying all accepted DEV** has the highest risk: it would replace a broad, unverified application source tree and could alter checkout, catalogue, pricing, staff, notifications, reports, or mobile behavior. Do not choose C. The first narrow feature slice to evaluate after a Production baseline exists is Refund Recording V1 (its reviewed migration plus only the required refund UI/actions); Refund Breakdown and Online Transfer wording can be separate follow-up slices. Preserve payment correction, legacy refunds, both checkout overloads, all existing records, and current Production catalogue/prices. Exclude Fresh Pick Order Cutoff, Ready-for-Collection Override, and Staff Administration V1.

**Regression gate before any future release:** Use read-only Production walkthroughs to capture current storefront/catalogue/cart, existing-order views, staff login/permissions, Settings/passkeys, reports, and mobile behavior. Use an isolated Production-shaped database and synthetic roles/orders for checkout, status transitions, payments/corrections, refund limits/idempotency, Fresh Pick stock/share-payment/cancellation, and reporting/export mutations. Verify the effective Production Supabase environment target without displaying secrets. Confirm no seed, reset, catalogue synchronization, or bulk migration command is in the release process. Any exact Production-only behavior discovered must be preserved or separately approved.

## Targeted public storefront failure diagnosis — 16 October 2026

**Current DEV deployment — VERIFIED:** Vercel resolves `dev.whitebird.asia` to READY deployment `dpl_ACBKzrDigZ4gVaLh8cJ2CS2XRkjo`, source `git`, branch `codex/cake-size-availability-dev`, commit `c6105eec0bbee64b86abcd45bb9be71f9eb2deeb`, with `dev.whitebird.asia` as an alias. The inspected DEV pages therefore correspond to the accepted commit; this is not a stale-deployment mismatch.

**Effective Supabase targets — UNKNOWN:** Public page content and Vercel deployment metadata do not prove the effective database URL/key pair. Prior non-decrypting environment metadata exposed encrypted values rather than a usable project reference; no value was decrypted, printed, or used. No cross-environment connection was detected, but the target remains unverified for both environments.

**DEV collection symptom — CONFIRMED; root cause UNKNOWN:** The accepted DEV `StorefrontCollectionCakesPage` concurrently loads monthly and special catalogue records, available cakes, special catalogues, and public vouchers before leaving its Suspense fallback. Available-cake processing can also call `library_cake_size_price_on` for effective prices. The observed “Loading collection” is that route's Suspense fallback, consistent with one server-side read remaining pending. A query error, wrong environment, schema/permission mismatch, or upstream latency remains possible. Vercel's aggregated runtime-error query returned no actionable cluster; grouped error-level route logs had sparse collection-route entries but no diagnostic text. No database query was run.

**DEV product-detail symptom — CONFIRMED; root cause UNKNOWN:** The accepted route resolves display metadata and live published-cake data separately. The live path reads commercial cake data and publication catalogues, applies effective pricing through `library_cake_size_price_on`, and loads offer/voucher data. Query helpers throw on Supabase errors, which can surface as a server-render error. The observed detail route returned a server-error screen, and Vercel grouped logs contained a sparse error-level entry for that route, but available summaries exposed no actionable cause. It may share an underlying target/network/database issue with collection loading because both use the public Supabase client and related catalogue/price reads; the different symptoms do not establish a common cause. Cross-environment routing, a missing object/permission, price RPC failure, and an application exception remain unproven alternatives.

**Production Browse Cakes — SINGLE OBSERVATION:** Production `/browse` remained on its loading fallback once, while the October/November collection pages and one cake detail rendered. The read-only Vercel runtime-error summary found no aggregated `/browse` error cluster in the selected window. This establishes neither a persistent defect nor successful completion. Production source and query behavior remain unavailable, so a route-specific diagnosis is unsupported.

**DEV product image placeholders — LIKELY DATA/METADATA DIFFERENCE:** In accepted DEV source, a cake card selects a listing image from its photo metadata and displays “Photo coming soon” when no image URL is selected. The image component has no equivalent text fallback for a failed network image request. That makes absent or unselected DEV photo metadata more consistent with the observed text than a failed request to a present URL. It does not distinguish missing photo rows, different URLs, unavailable Storage objects, or selection/mapping differences. No URL, Storage object, or catalogue record was inspected; do not synchronize media or catalogue data.

**Confirmed versus unresolved:** Confirmed: the accepted commit serves DEV; the two DEV collection routes showed loading fallbacks; the DEV product detail showed a server error; Production Browse showed a loading fallback once; and two DEV homepage cards showed placeholders while Production showed images. Not confirmed: the failing query, any cross-project connection, a persistent Production Browse defect, underlying image object/URL state, or a shared root cause. Vercel's pre-aggregated runtime-error lookup returned no clusters for queried storefront paths; sparse route-grouped error logs did not identify a cause.

**Minimum corrective scope / next step:** Make no code, database, or media changes yet. First obtain a safe, non-secret confirmation of the effective Supabase project reference for the exact DEV deployment. Then correlate timestamped requests for the failing DEV routes with sanitized Vercel and verified-DEV Supabase/PostgREST logs, retaining only route, status, error class, and object/function name. Recheck Production Browse once in a later observation window before classifying it as a defect. No fix, migration, data synchronization, deployment, commit, or push was performed.

## Production behavior baseline — public storefront observation — 16 October 2026

**Environment — PARTIALLY VERIFIED:** The existing in-app browser loaded the public homepages at `https://whitebird.asia` and `https://dev.whitebird.asia`; the visible hostnames match the stated Production and DEV domains. The effective Supabase project target was not safely observable from these public pages and remains **UNKNOWN** for both environments. No credentials or tokens were inspected. An existing DEV tab was a Customer Operations preview populated with demonstration content, not an authenticated live staff session; it was not treated as a production behavior baseline.

**Page-load safety — LIMITED:** Only public GET pages were opened. No buttons, cart additions, forms, checkout, staff actions, or report downloads were used. The exact Production application source is unavailable, so hidden page-load effects such as view tracking or background synchronization cannot be ruled out. Authenticated order, payment, refund, Settings, passkey, report, and Fresh Pick operations screens were therefore not opened. No business action was initiated by this audit; server-side effects of page views remain unverified.

**Screens actually observed — VERIFIED:** Production homepage; Production October 2026 collection; Production November 2026 collection; Production Thai Milk Tea Mango product detail. DEV homepage; DEV October and November collection routes; DEV Thai Milk Tea Mango product detail. Production `/browse` remained at “Loading cakes.” DEV collection routes also remained at “Loading collection,” and the DEV product detail displayed “This page couldn’t load” with a server error. These are live observations only and do not identify their cause.

**Observed Production public baseline — VERIFIED:** The homepage displayed October and November collection sections, Fresh Picks and Popular Cakes, with product names, variants/prices, preorder lead times and links. The October collection page loaded and showed 17 cakes with search/category/size/price/preorder/sort controls. The November collection page loaded and showed 18 cakes. The Thai Milk Tea Mango detail showed 4-inch and 6-inch variants, price/lead-time text, and an availability notice that the 4-inch size becomes available from 1 November 2026; no variant or “Add to Order” action was activated. No cart or checkout screen was inspected. This is a point-in-time display snapshot, not verification of scheduled-price transitions, checkout totals, stock reservations, delivery charges, or order submission.

**Observed DEV comparison — VERIFIED / UNKNOWN:** The DEV homepage displayed the same visible page structure and featured October/November products and displayed prices in the items observed. Two November homepage cards showed “Photo coming soon” in DEV while corresponding Production cards showed cake imagery. This is a confirmed public display difference; whether it is intended or reflects DEV catalogue/media data is **UNKNOWN** and must not be resolved by copying DEV catalogue records into Production. DEV October/November collection routes did not finish loading, and the DEV product-detail route returned an application server error, so collection contents, variants, availability, and details could not be compared live. No price or product-data equivalence is established beyond the homepage items visibly compared.

**Staff and operational baseline — UNKNOWN:** Production login, dashboard, existing orders, payment display, refund interface, Fresh Pick operations, Settings, passkey management, reports, Refund Breakdown, exports, and mobile staff navigation were not inspected. There was no authenticated Production session in the existing browser. The DEV preview route is synthetic and cannot prove live operational behavior. The earlier source/schema evidence about Production-specific `record_payment_correction` and the additional checkout overload remains a release-protection requirement, not proof of what the UI currently does.

**Release risk and protection — HIGH:** The live DEV collection/detail errors block using the current public DEV environment as a verified comparison for those pages; investigate as a DEV-only acceptance issue before relying on it. The effective Supabase targets and all authenticated workflows remain unverified. Preserve Production orders/statuses, payments/refunds, catalogue, variants, scheduled/current prices, Fresh Pick stock/reservations, and customer/staff accounts as authoritative. Do not seed or synchronize DEV data, restore historical data, or use destructive operations. Preserve the known Production-specific payment-correction behavior and both checkout overloads. Exclude Fresh Pick Order Cutoff, Ready-for-Collection Override, and Staff Administration V1.

**Minimum release scope and next action:** The previous recommendation remains selective integration onto a trusted Production source baseline; full DEV replacement is not supported by this observation. Before preparing a candidate, the next exact action is to resolve the DEV public collection/detail loading failures and repeat only those read-only public comparisons. Authenticated Production walkthroughs remain pending a known-safe page-load assessment and an already authorized Production staff session; do not perform operational actions. No data was synchronized, no forms were submitted, and no deployment, migration, or code change occurred in this audit.

The two routes share public Supabase reads and pricing/catalogue paths in source, so a common slow dependency is plausible but unproven. ## DEV storefront timeout follow-up — 16 October 2026

**DEV log query — BLOCKED:** One read-only Supabase unified-log query for 16 October 2026 06:37–06:39 UTC failed at the logging backend with “Backend error! Retry your query.” Per instruction, it was not retried. No DEV Postgres/PostgREST log record was available to correlate with the Vercel events.

**Vercel correlation — VERIFIED:** The previously captured raw error-level function records are at 06:37:47 UTC for `/order/collection/<id>` and 06:38:09 UTC for `/cakes/<id>`; both contain timeout/abort evidence. The collection request cannot be assigned to October or November from the available metadata. The logs do not name a Supabase relation, function, permission error, or request ID.

**Read-only DEV database snapshot — VERIFIED AT 07:06 UTC ONLY:** `statement_timeout` was 120,000 ms, `max_connections` was 60, and `lock_timeout` was 0. At the second `pg_stat_activity` snapshot, 22 client backends were idle; there were no active client queries over 30 seconds, no open client transaction reported, and no lock waiters. This later, point-in-time snapshot does not establish database health at 06:37–06:39 UTC and does not indicate connection exhaustion or blocking at the time inspected. No sessions were terminated and no settings were changed.

**Representative query plans — VERIFIED, non-executing:** Plain `EXPLAIN (FORMAT JSON)` (without `ANALYZE`) was run for representative catalogue-list, collection-membership, and cake/size lookups using a synthetic UUID. The collection-membership plan used `collection_cakes_collection_id_idx` and applied availability filtering and sorting; the cake-size relation used `library_cake_sizes_cake_id_idx`. Planner estimates for the collection list and cake lookup were small enough for sequential scans. Relevant indexes also exist for collection ID, cake membership, collection status/month/display order, cake primary key, size-by-cake, and effective price rows. These representative plans provide no evidence of a missing index or an expensive scan as the incident cause. They are not the exact PostgREST-generated plans for every embedded selection. No `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` was run.

**Accepted source query path — VERIFIED AT COMMIT `c6105eec`:** The collection route awaits five tasks concurrently: monthly catalogue lookup, special-catalogue lookup, collection cakes, special catalogues, and public catalogue vouchers. The cake-list read embeds library cake data through `collection_cakes`, then applies effective prices by calling `library_cake_size_price_on` once per size in concurrent batches of 20; successive batches run one after another. Cake detail starts cached display data and live commercial/catalogue data concurrently. The live path reads one cake with its size rows and available collection memberships; it then resolves effective price per size through the same pricing RPC. The detail display path separately loads cake photos/tags/assignments with compatibility fallbacks. These are potential latency amplifiers, especially the per-size RPC fan-out, but no log identifies one as the request that stalled.

**Timeout and error handling — VERIFIED:** The accepted server/public Supabase clients wrap fetches with `AbortSignal.timeout`; the default data-fetch timeout is 8 seconds. The route source throws on Supabase query errors, with limited legacy-column retries when a specific optional column is missing. A slow Supabase fetch can therefore produce an abort and leave the collection Suspense fallback or trigger the detail error boundary. This is consistent with the observed timeout/abort messages, but does not prove which read was slow or whether Supabase, network, Vercel, or application query fan-out initiated it.

**Indexes and root cause — UNKNOWN / no demonstrated index defect:** There is no evidence from the available logs or representative plans that a missing index caused the timeouts. Historical database activity at 06:37–06:39 is unavailable. Missing catalogue data, RLS errors, failed pricing RPCs, and the identity of the slow request also remain unverified. No code or database correction is justified from the current evidence.

**Production incident relationship — UNKNOWN:** The reported Production statement-timeout/aborted-transaction incident occurred around 06:59 UTC, about 20–22 minutes after the DEV timeout records. The incidents have different evidence: the DEV Vercel logs show application/function timeout-abort symptoms, while the reported Production errors are database-side. Temporal proximity alone does not establish shared infrastructure or a common cause. Production was not accessed during this follow-up.

**Next exact action:** When the DEV collection failure is next observed, capture one timestamped read-only request and its Vercel request ID, then inspect the corresponding DEV Supabase API/Postgres log window once through the dashboard. Use that paired evidence to identify the specific slow query/RPC before considering an application or index change. No writes, code changes, deployment, commit, or push were performed.

## Production release baseline update — owner-reported browser observations — 16 October 2026

**Latest DEV public behavior — OWNER-OBSERVED / REPORTED:** At approximately 16:59–17:00 Malaysia time, the owner used normal Mac Safari. The October collection loaded with 17 cakes, names/categories, preorder information, prices, and search/filter controls. Thai Milk Tea Mango detail loaded with its description, 4-inch RM78 and 6-inch RM135 prices, 4-inch availability from 1 November 2026, and preorder information. No Add to Order or checkout action was used. These point-in-time observations establish that both previously failing routes can succeed; they do not establish continuous stability or explain the earlier timeout/abort logs.

**Photo and catalogue authority — VERIFIED AGREEMENT:** Production remains authoritative for cake lists, variants, October/November catalogues, current/scheduled prices, and photos. Catalogue data was previously synchronized one-way from Production to DEV. Photo synchronization remains deliberately postponed due to image transformation limits. Missing DEV photos are expected and are not an application regression by themselves. No data or Storage synchronization is part of this release; Production must continue to use its existing catalogue, prices, and photo objects.

**Production operational incident — OWNER-REPORTED WITH VERCEL LOG EVIDENCE; ROOT CAUSE UNKNOWN:** At approximately 14:59 Malaysia time on 16 October, Production Staff Home displayed a server error (digest `2564154980`). Vercel logs showed an aborted transaction message and a database statement timeout. The owner later observed Staff Home loading in both Private and normal Safari, with order and Fresh Pick information; the Supabase dashboard reported Healthy. This was a real intermittent incident that recovered. It is not attributed to Safari, Supabase compute size, or recent DEV changes. Treat it as an unresolved operational stability risk: it does not alone impose an indefinite release hold while the service is healthy, but require a healthy pre-release window, monitoring during/after release, and STOP if transaction-abort or timeout symptoms recur.

### Minimal release feasibility and sequencing

**Strategy assessment:**

- **Full accepted DEV deployment — NOT FEASIBLE SAFELY NOW.** It would replace a broad application whose differences from Production are unknown and risks changing checkout, catalogues/pricing behavior, staff flows, notifications, and operations.
- **Selective feature integration — preferred in principle, but NOT FEASIBLE until a trustworthy Production source baseline exists.** Do not create a selective patch on the non-authoritative historical candidate or assume DEV is the base.
- **Minimal release patch — also NOT FEASIBLE now.** A small diff still needs an exact base to know what behavior it replaces. Once a maintained Production source tree is identified, the smallest useful first slice is Refund Recording V1 alone (reviewed Production-specific migration plus only its required application UI/actions); handle reports/exports/terminology and staff/mobile improvements as later independent slices if they are confirmed needed.

**Data-preservation review:** The accepted DEV package build/start scripts were previously inspected: they use `next build`/`next start`; no automatic migration or seed hook was found in prebuild/postinstall/instrumentation, and `vercel.json` has no cron declaration. Explicit seed scripts exist and must not run in Production. No automatic bulk replacement of business data was identified in that accepted source/configuration. This does not establish Production Vercel build-command overrides, Production scheduled jobs, external integrations, or behavior of the unavailable current Production source. Normal checkout/order/payment/refund actions can create legitimate future business changes; test mutations only with synthetic data in isolation. Never sync DEV data, restore the October 14 backup over current Production, seed Production, reset/truncate tables, or replace catalogue, pricing, Storage, staff, or customer records.

**Required ordering if a release becomes eligible:** (1) establish the exact maintained Production source and its production-only behavior; (2) verify deployment-scoped Production configuration and ensure build/deploy has no automatic migration or seed step; (3) test the selected app slice against an isolated Production-compatible database with synthetic users/orders/payments/refunds/inventory; (4) complete a current recovery plan and owner acceptance of the partial restore-rehearsal limits; (5) after a separate explicit approval, apply only the reviewed Refund V1 migration atomically, with immediate post-migration catalog/permission checks; (6) deploy the matching minimal app slice to Production; (7) run read-only smoke checks and monitor Vercel/Supabase health. The migration must precede application code that calls its new columns/RPCs. Before migrating, establish that the current Production application and external callers do not rely on direct `anon`/`authenticated` refund-table writes removed by the reviewed security correction. Never run `supabase db push` or reconcile unrelated histories.

**Rollback and recovery limits:** Reverting application code would leave the database migration in place. Do not restore the October 14 backup over live Production: it predates current orders, payments, catalogue/prices, stock, and accounts. A safe rollback needs a verified previous application artifact plus a reviewed forward/compensating database plan that preserves any refunds recorded after release. Full Supabase restore readiness remains **BLOCKED / incomplete**, so no claim of complete disaster recovery is made.

**Release STOP conditions:** Stop before any Production change if the exact app source/baseline is still unknown; deployment-specific Supabase target or build override is unverified; the selected diff touches unrelated or deferred behavior; the migration checksum/schema/permission preflight changes; a current app/external direct refund writer cannot be accounted for; the required recovery limitation is not accepted; any seed, reset, DEV-to-Production sync, automatic migration, or unrelated function replacement is in the command path; synthetic regression checks fail; or timeouts/transaction-abort symptoms recur during the release window. No migration or deployment is authorized by this status update.

**Current release decision — BLOCKED:** The application replacement is not ready for Production. The owner-reported DEV route successes improve the acceptance baseline but do not resolve Production source, application compatibility, direct-write caller, effective deployment configuration, or full recovery gaps. The Production incident is recovered but warrants targeted monitoring rather than an unsupported root-cause claim.

**One next executable step:** Have the owner identify or provide the exact maintained Production source tree intended for the next build (a commit or source archive), without restarting historical Vercel/Git recovery. Use that trusted tree to prepare a narrowly scoped Refund Recording V1 diff; do not create a release branch, apply the migration, or deploy until that baseline and its direct-refund call sites are reviewed.

### Minimum release design and feasibility

**Best available source baseline:** Use `bc2b2c37432161c22ddcfd280223c1eda4c65d56` only as a preserved Production-oriented anchor for constructing a test candidate. It is a real, available source tree and a known ancestor of accepted DEV, but is not verified as the exact October 13 deployment. Do not use the entire accepted DEV tree as a presumed replacement baseline.

**Preserve or reconstruct documented Production reporting:** The October 13 release record's Management Reports V1.0 scope is represented by available descendant source: monthly reports commit `1570f38...`, management/report navigation commits `06fcb0e...` and `89d5a84...`, and month/mobile navigation commits `6f7b1ea...`, `7d95275...`, and `ab0acdc...`. This supports reconstruction of the documented report sections from source, but live Production parity remains unknown. Reports V1.1 exports (`364eb155...`) and Refund Breakdown (`c6105eec...`) are separate additions.

**Known database compatibility requirements:** Refund V1 requires the reviewed Production-specific migration for refund metadata, type/idempotency support, and three V1 RPCs. Payment-correction source must preserve Production's existing `record_payment_correction` body. Preserve both Production `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads. Cake-size availability has three separate migrations listed above and is not part of the minimum Refund V1 slice unless explicitly selected. Fresh Pick override migration is already in the `bc2` source tree, creating the scope conflict above. Reports V1.0 itself contains no migration in its introducing commit.

**Unknowns and release risks:** Exact October 13 application source and uncommitted deployment-time changes remain unavailable. Current Production UI behavior for many accepted DEV features remains unknown. Production-specific checkout/payment bodies and application call sites must be tested together. Passkey/Auth project configuration, external integrations, current catalogue/prices/photos, Storage objects, and Production runtime configuration remain outside this source-only inventory. Production business records stay authoritative; no DEV data, seed, backup restore, catalogue, price, or Storage synchronization is allowed.

**Narrow candidate feasibility:** A test-only candidate can be assembled from the available `bc2` anchor plus individually reviewed source commits; the exact missing October 13 commit is not required to create such a candidate. It cannot be called a faithful replacement or released safely based on source ancestry alone. Before assembly, resolve the Ready Override scope conflict and choose feature commits/migrations individually. Then test against isolated Production-shaped schema/data using synthetic records and perform the previously identified storefront, operations, staff, payments, Fresh Pick, reports, exports, and mobile regression matrix. No candidate was created in this inventory.

**One next exact action:** Resolve the Ready Override boundary: decide whether the exclusion applies only to the separate deferred candidate or also requires removing the older implementation already contained in `bc2`; until that is explicit, do not assemble a candidate from that baseline.

## Ready Override release-boundary resolution — owner decision — 16 October 2026

**Owner decision — VERIFIED:** Include accepted Fresh Pick Share Payment. Exclude the separate deferred Ready-for-Collection Override candidate. Do not automatically remove the older Ready Override code in `bc2`; retain it only if that will not newly expose the feature or require an unapproved migration.

**Older implementation in `bc2` — VERIFIED FROM SOURCE:** Available commit `14c9288e670c4541c7972d3be632a1790471e9b5` is an ancestor of `bc2` and accepted DEV. Its migration `20261005234831_fresh_pick_ready_for_collection_override.sql` adds `extra_stock.ready_for_collection`, defines `mark_extra_stock_ready_for_collection(uuid, uuid, text)` and `undo_extra_stock_ready_for_collection(uuid, uuid, text)`, and replaces `_assert_fresh_picks_customer_fulfilment` so ready physical stock can bypass only the same-day preparation cutoff for pickup/dine-in while other checks remain. The RPCs bind the staff actor, allow Bakery/Manager/Owner, lock the exact stock row, validate state, and record stock events. The migration grants execution to authenticated/service_role and revokes it from public/anon.

**Older implementation reachability — VERIFIED IN SOURCE:** The full Bakery EXTRA board renders “Mark Ready for Collection” and “Undo Ready status” controls when `canMarkReadyForCollection` / `canUndoReadyForCollection` is true. Those capabilities are assigned to roles allowed to mutate Bakery stock; server actions recheck the capability and call the RPCs. Storefront query/cart and fulfilment code carry `ready_for_collection` and use it for the qualifying pickup/dine-in eligibility bypass. This is active, reachable code in the `bc2` and accepted DEV trees, not dormant code or a flag-protected feature.

**Separate deferred candidate — UNAVAILABLE FOR FILE-LEVEL COMPARISON:** The supplied `9ad16bc...` reference remains a partial hash with no matching local object/ref. The previously listed `/private/tmp/project-flour-ready-dev` worktree path is absent. No full hash or other source snapshot was available, so its application files, RPCs, and migration cannot be compared with `14c9288...`. Do not equate the two implementations merely because both concern Ready for Collection.

**Fresh Pick Share Payment dependency review — VERIFIED FROM SOURCE:** The Share Payment enablement in `14c9288...` is implemented in the payment context helper and `PaymentRequestPreview`: a customer-website Fresh Pick can use the wholecake preorder QR context, and an awaiting-payment order with a positive balance can share the payment request. The focused test covers Fresh Pick pickup, delivery, dine-in, and outstanding-balance cases. This payment-context change itself does not read `ready_for_collection` or call either Ready RPC, and the commit contains no database migration specific to the QR/payment-context change.

**Shared-commit coupling — VERIFIED FROM SOURCE:** Commit `14c9288...` also changes shared Fresh Pick fulfilment, cart, storefront query/form, Bakery board/action, and capability files to propagate and act on `ready_for_collection`; it adds the Ready migration above. Therefore the commit cannot be taken wholesale to include Share Payment while excluding Ready Override. The narrow Share Payment behavior can be isolated from the payment context helper/preview and its tests, but the shared files must be reviewed against their parent to avoid dropping required payment behavior or accidentally retaining Ready-specific code. The Ready migration and ready-flag query/eligibility path are not prerequisites for the Share Payment QR context change.

**Production migration status — OBJECTS VERIFIED; HISTORY STATUS UNKNOWN:** Existing October 14 Production schema evidence includes `extra_stock.ready_for_collection` and the two mark/undo RPC definitions; the prior October 16 read-only Production catalog comparison also recorded both RPCs as present and matching the accepted definitions. Thus the migration's relevant schema effects were already present in the inspected Production snapshot, and a fresh application of this migration is not indicated by available evidence. The migration-history row/version registration was not verified. No Production connection or change was made for this boundary review.

The owner’s 16 October Production Safari observation now confirms the Mark Ready for Collection control is already exposed on Bakery → Fresh Pick. The owner did not activate it. Therefore UI exposure is not a new gap to solve; whether the action succeeds and its exact runtime behavior remain unverified. ### Minimum genuine release scope and compatibility

**Confirmed gap:** Refund Recording V1 is the only listed accepted DEV feature with a verified Production schema gap. Its minimum functional slice is the reviewed V1 RPCs and metadata/idempotency migration plus only the application paths required to record and display those refunds. The itemized Refund Breakdown may be included if it is needed to expose the newly recorded detail, but it depends on the same V1 schema and must reconcile without replacing Production's existing Management Reports sections.

**Not established as gaps:** Reports V1.1 exports, Share Payment, Add to Order V2, credential/passkey changes, staff identity display, and the iPhone Operations fix have unknown Production status. Do not include them in the minimum release merely because they exist in DEV. A targeted observation is only justified if the owner needs one of those features in the same release; there is no evidence to label them missing.

**Preserve exactly:** Production's `record_payment_correction` body; both `submit_guest_extra_order` overloads; legacy overpayment and Fresh Pick cancellation/refund workflows; existing Ready Override UI/RPCs and authorization; current report V1.0 behavior; and all live customer/order/payment/refund/catalogue/pricing/stock/account data. No DEV data, seeds, historical restore, catalogue, price, photo or Storage synchronization is permitted.

**Database and application ordering:** The V1 migration is not applied. If separately approved later, apply only the checksum-reviewed Production-specific migration before deploying the V1 application code that calls its new RPCs/columns. Do not run general DEV migration synchronization. Verify the migration's exact target/version and preserved function bodies immediately before execution. No migration or application deployment is authorized by this feature-gap assessment.

**Source and candidate feasibility:** The exact current Production application source remains unavailable. `bc2b2c...` is a preserved Production-oriented anchor, not a verified deployed snapshot. A test-only candidate can be assembled from that anchor and individually selected accepted changes, but cannot be treated as a faithful Production replacement. The old Ready implementation is already in the anchor and the screenshot verifies Production UI presence; retain it rather than trying to strip it. Do not bring in the separate deferred candidate or its changes. Use isolated testing to verify selected integration against a Production-shaped schema with synthetic data; do not test mutations against live Production records.

### Remaining release blockers

1. **HIGH — Exact application baseline unknown:** The October 13 deployed source is not verified. This blocks a claim of parity or a broad whole-tree replacement, though it does not erase the concrete Refund V1 schema gap.
2. **HIGH — Refund V1 not released:** The reviewed migration remains unapplied and requires separate explicit execution authorization; V1 application code must not be deployed ahead of its schema.
3. **HIGH — Recovery readiness incomplete:** Full Supabase disaster recovery remains blocked. Never use the October 14 backup to overwrite current Production; business data newer than that snapshot is authoritative.
4. **MEDIUM/HIGH — Unverified feature behavior:** Several accepted DEV features have unknown Production status. Keep them out of a minimal release unless a bounded inspection establishes a need.
5. **MEDIUM — Operational stability:** The transient 16 October Production timeout/aborted-transaction incident is unresolved. Require healthy pre-release checks and stop if the error recurs; do not reopen broad diagnosis absent new evidence.

**One next executable step:** Prepare a non-deployable, isolated Refund Recording V1 integration candidate from the preserved source and reviewed V1 changes only, retaining Production-specific payment correction, both checkout overloads, legacy refund paths, report V1.0, and existing Ready UI/RPCs. Do not execute the migration, alter business data, commit, push, or deploy as part of that preparation.

## Refund Recording V1 Production release sequencing gate — 16 October 2026

**Scope:** Read-only sequencing assessment of the reviewed migration `20261015010528_refund_recording_v1.sql`, SHA-256 `0f0792dcb87d2a5092b16d335b7149d349ac3ae260c81cd93cb7bfccfda66b64`. No database or application action was performed. This assessment relies on the already recorded 16 October Production preflight and isolated PostgreSQL 17.11 rehearsal; it does not re-query current Production state or repeat the tests.

**Database-only feasibility — TECHNICALLY BACKWARD-COMPATIBLE IN TESTED SCOPE; NOT CLEARED FOR PRODUCTION EXECUTION:** The reviewed migration adds nullable refund metadata, supports the additional `order_refund` type while retaining the existing refund types, adds request-key idempotency, installs the three V1 RPCs, replaces the refund validator, and revokes `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, and `TRUNCATE` from `anon` and `authenticated` on `public.refunds`. It retains refund `SELECT`, does not change `service_role`/`postgres` privileges, and the recorded preflight found no PUBLIC or inherited-role DML grants. The current authenticated SELECT policy and RLS configuration are not changed. The migration applied atomically in the isolated PostgreSQL 17.11 rehearsal against the restored October 14 schema and rows; this is compatibility evidence for that snapshot, not proof about current rows or traffic.

**Existing Production workflows — PRESERVATION EVIDENCE:** The read-only 16 October preflight found the migration unapplied, its expected fields and V1 RPCs absent, and no relevant definition drift from the October 14 snapshot among the inspected objects. The reviewed migration does not replace `record_payment_correction`, the legacy overpayment refund RPC, the Fresh Pick cancellation/refund RPC, either `submit_guest_extra_order` overload, or the Ready-for-Collection RPCs. Prior isolated regression evidence says those protected functions and checkout overloads remained present/unchanged and their tested paths worked. The existing refund `SELECT` grant/policy remains. The replacement validator and expanded type constraint are designed to preserve the two existing refund categories; applying against restored historical rows succeeded. These results do not establish behavior for every current Production row or caller.

**Direct-writer compatibility — UNRESOLVED RELEASE BLOCKER:** Production previously granted client roles table-level DML, but its refund table had RLS enabled, FORCE RLS off, and only an authenticated SELECT policy. Ordinary `anon`/`authenticated` INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE therefore had no matching RLS write policy already; TRUNCATE is not controlled by RLS. The migration removes those explicit DML/TRUNCATE grants while preserving SELECT. The accepted DEV application-source review found no direct refund-table writes. The exact deployed Production application source is unavailable, and external integrations/operational scripts have not been inventoried. `service_role` retains direct table privileges and BYPASSRLS; its external callers remain unverified and are unaffected by the grant revocation, although the new validator/constraints would still govern writes that fire the refund trigger. Existing legacy refund operations are expected to continue through their preserved privileged RPCs, but that does not prove all current callers use those RPCs or that no service-role writer depends on prior validation behavior. Read-only evidence needed before deciding this gate: an owner-designated maintained Production application source/build inventory and its refund call sites, plus an inventory of operational scripts and external integrations that can write refunds. Confirm callers use the intended RPCs or another explicitly retained path; do not infer this from accepted DEV source or database grants alone.

**Operational and recovery risk — HIGH:** Production experienced intermittent transaction-abort/statement-timeout symptoms on 16 October and recovered; root cause remains unknown. Do not run a schema change during instability or on recurrence. The migration was atomic in rehearsal, but Production lock duration and contention were not measured; DDL/index work can hold locks and should only be scheduled after a bounded operational plan establishes a quiet window, lock/statement timeout behavior, monitoring, and an abort response. Full Supabase disaster recovery remains incomplete: the October 14 backup is older than live business data, its local restore had Supabase-managed dependency and ownership/ACL limitations, and it must never be restored over current Production. A recovery plan must preserve all orders, payments, refunds, catalogue/prices, stock, accounts, and newer records; application rollback alone will not reverse the migration.

### Sequencing decision

| Option | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **A — Migration first, application later** | **Not approved as safe yet.** The schema is designed to tolerate the existing RPC paths, but a schema-only change would also remove client-role DML grants before the current Production application and outside callers have been identified. It could leave the old app running against a changed permission surface without a verified rollback/recovery path. |
| **B — Application and migration together** | **Not currently feasible safely.** It avoids a long schema/app gap, but exact Production application source is unavailable, so a trustworthy minimal app diff cannot yet be assembled or verified. A paired release also does not answer whether external callers rely on the removed grants. |
| **C — Defer both until caller and source compatibility is established** | **RECOMMENDED.** Keep the reviewed migration unchanged and unapplied. First establish a trustworthy maintained source/caller inventory and resolve the recovery/operational gate; then prepare and test a bounded paired release while preserving Production-specific functions and data. |

**Exact STOP conditions before any future execution:** stop if the migration hash differs; Production project identity or migration-absent status is not freshly verified; relevant schema/ACL/RLS/function definitions drift from the reviewed preflight; any current application, integration, or script may depend on direct client-role refund writes and its path is unresolved; an unexpected migration dependency or object change is found; Production shows timeout/aborted-transaction or other instability; an atomic execution and lock/monitoring plan is unavailable; recovery limitations have not been explicitly accepted with a current-data-preserving response plan; or the proposed scope includes unapproved source, permission, function, data, seed, synchronization, or deployment changes.

**Decision:** Do not apply this migration independently now. The rehearsal supports bounded database compatibility, but not safe independent rollout against an unknown live caller set and incomplete recovery readiness. Keep the application unchanged and the migration unapplied pending those gates. No migration or deployment is authorized by this record.

**One next executable step:** Obtain the owner-designated maintained Production application source and a bounded inventory of refund-writing operational/external callers, then verify every identified writer’s path against the migration’s client-role revocations without touching Production data.
